1 Holomorphic Functions
1.1
REVIEW OF COMPLEX NUMBERS
I had an exhausting dream, he said the moment I sat down by
him one morning. I dreamed that I was a negative number,
and I couldn’t figure out my square root.
It’s possible, I said. Wait until you learn the imaginary number.
Mommy, I’m not stupid, he said. I know imaginary numbers,
but I don’t like to deal with that troublesome i.
YIYUN LI, Where Reasons End
The set ℂof complex numbers consists of all pairs (𝑎, 𝑏) ∈ℝ2 with addition defined by
(𝑎, 𝑏) + (𝑎′, 𝑏′) = (𝑎+ 𝑎′, 𝑏+ 𝑏′)
and multiplication by
(𝑎, 𝑏) ⋅(𝑎′, 𝑏′) = (𝑎𝑎′ −𝑏𝑏′, 𝑎𝑏′ + 𝑎′𝑏) .
We set 𝑖= (0, 1) and write suggestively
𝑎+ 𝑏𝑖= (𝑎, 𝑏) .
Then 𝑖2 = −1. Moreover, real part, imaginary part, conjugate and norm are defined as
Re(𝑎+ 𝑏𝑖) = 𝑎
Im(𝑎+ 𝑏𝑖) = 𝑏
𝑎+ 𝑏𝑖= 𝑎−𝑏𝑖
|𝑎+ 𝑏𝑖| = √
⁡
(𝑎+ 𝑏𝑖)(𝑎+ 𝑏𝑖) = √
⁡
𝑎2 + 𝑏2
The norm is multiplicative
|𝑧⋅𝑧′| = |𝑧| ⋅|𝑧′| ,
which allows to compute the reciprocal or multiplicative inverse of a complex number as
1⁄
𝑧= 𝑧
⁄
|𝑧|2 .
With all this, ℂbecomes a commutative field, and we can define elementary complex functions:
1.
𝑓(𝑧) = 𝑧+ 𝑎is a translation by 𝑎.
2.
𝑓(𝑧) = 𝑎𝑧scales 𝑧by 𝑎with center the origin.
3.
𝑓(𝑧) = 𝑒𝑖𝑡𝑧rotates 𝑧by the angle 𝑡.
We also have polynomials

1.2
HOLOMORPHIC FUNCTIONS
Definition 1.2.1 Let 𝑈⊂ℂbe an open set, and 𝑓: 𝑈→ℂa complex-valued function. We say that 𝑓is holomorphic at
𝑧0 ∈𝑈if
𝑓′(𝑧0) = lim
𝑧→𝑧0
𝑓(𝑧) −𝑓(𝑧0)
⁄
𝑧−𝑧0
exists. We say that 𝑓is holomorphic in 𝑈if it is holomorphic at every point of 𝑈. The set of all holomorphic functions in 𝑈
is denoted by 𝒪(𝑈).
Constants and the identity function are trivially holomorphic. Our first two goals will be to construct examples and
to determine how holomorphic functions are more special than real differentiable functions.
This definition is formally analogous to the definition of the differentiability of a real-valued function at a point
𝑥0 ∈ℝ. We therefore expect all elementary properties of the derivative to hold, with literally identical proofs.
In particular:
Theorem 1.2.2 The sum, product, and quotient of holomorphic functions are again complex differentiable whenever defined.
Proof. Clear.
This shows that all polynomials and all rational functions are holomorphic.
Example 1.2.1
1.
𝑓(𝑧) = 𝑎𝑧is a rotation by arg 𝑎and scaling by |𝑎|.
2.
𝑓(𝑧) = 𝑧+ 𝑏is a translation.
3.
𝑓(𝑧) = 1/𝑧is an inversion at the unit circle followed by a reflection at the real axis. It maps lines and circles to
lines or circles.

1.4
INVERSION
The geometry of the reciprocal 𝑧↦1/𝑧is intimately related to the inversion (or reflection) at the unit circle
𝐼(𝑧) = 𝑧
⁄
|𝑧|2 = 1⁄
𝑧;
they only differ by a conjugation, which is the reflection at the real axis. We claim
Theorem 1.4.1 The inversion (and thus the reciprocal as well) maps
1. lines through 0 to lines through 0;
2. lines not through 0 to circles through 0;
3. circles not through 0 to circles not through 0.
Proof. The case of a line through 0 is trivial.
We begin with the special case of the vertical line through 1. By Thales, the angle at 𝑞is a right angle. Because the
triangles Δ(0, 1, 𝑞′) and Δ(0, 𝑞, 1) are similar, we have |𝑞| ⋅|𝑞′| = 1 so that 𝑞lies on the line Re 𝑧= 1 if and only if 𝑞′
lies on the circle with center at 1/2 and radius 1/2.
Figure 1.2
Inverting line to circle
Note that this implies that any line that touches the unit circle is mapped by 𝐼to a circle touching the unit circle at
the same point as the line, as 𝐼is equivariant with respect to rotations.
Next we show that the inversion takes any vertical line to a circle. To see this, let 𝑅(𝑧) = 𝑟⋅𝑧for a fixed positive real
number. Then 𝐼= 𝑅∘𝐼∘𝑅. Given a line 𝐿not through 0, find 𝑟so that 𝑅(𝐿) touches the circle. Then 𝐼(𝐿) = 𝑅∘𝐼∘𝑅(𝐿)
is indeed a circle, by the previous step.

1.5
CHAIN RULES
We note that all Möbius transformations preserve angles. Let's prove this in general.

𝑓(𝛾(𝑡)) has tangent vectors 𝑓′(𝛾(𝑡)) ⋅𝛾′(𝑡). Note that the product here is a complex multiplication.

1.6
CAUCHY-RIEMANN EQUATIONS
Lemma 1.6.1 (Cauchy-Riemann Equations) Let
𝑓(𝑥+ 𝑖𝑦) = 𝑢(𝑥, 𝑦) + 𝑖𝑣(𝑥, 𝑦)
be complex differentiable. Then
𝑢𝑥= 𝑣𝑦
and
𝑢𝑦= −𝑣𝑥.
Proof. Let ℎ∈ℝ. Taking the limit ℎ→0 in

⁄

𝑓′(𝑧) = 𝑣𝑦(𝑥, 𝑦) −𝑖𝑢𝑦(𝑥, 𝑦) .
Comparing both right-hand sides proves the claim.
These two equations are called the Cauchy-Riemann equations.
Note that if 𝑓′ is continuous, so are 𝑢𝑥, 𝑢𝑦, 𝑣𝑥, and 𝑣𝑦.
We now prove a converse:
Lemma 1.6.2

equations 1.6.1 and such that the map

Proof. Write
𝑢(𝑥+ ℎ, 𝑦+ 𝑘) = 𝑢(𝑥, 𝑦) + 𝑢𝑥ℎ+ 𝑢𝑦𝑘+ 𝑅(ℎ, 𝑘)

with real valued functions 𝑅and 𝑆. Write Δ = ℎ+ 𝑖𝑘. By assumption,
lim

so that 𝑓′ exists.

1.7
PATH INTEGRALS
An important tool in analysis to construct new functions is integration. For instance, the logarithm can be introduced as
an antiderivative of 1/𝑥, and the exponential function as its inverse. Our goal is to introduce an integral for holomorphic
functions that serves as an antiderivative.
Definition 1.7.1 Let 𝛾: [𝑎, 𝑏] →𝑈be a piecewise continuously differentiable curve, and 𝑓holomorphic in 𝑈. Define the

More generally: Suppose 𝑓has a holomorphic antiderivative 𝐹in 𝑈, i.e. 𝐹′(𝑧) = 𝑓(𝑧). Suppose that 𝛾: [𝑎, 𝑏] →𝑈

∫

While this is expected from the real analogy, it is also surprising because it shows that the integral of a function
with an antiderivative depends only on the endpoints of the path, not on the path itself. We are aiming to show
this for general holomorphic functions without assuming the existence of an antiderivative. At the moment, we see
that 1/𝑧has no antiderivative in any domain containing the unit circle.
We will frequently need to estimate path integrals. A simple but essential tool is the following lemma:

1.9

Now we can construct new functions by integration. For instance, if 𝑓is holomorphic (with continuous derivative) in
a star-shaped domain, we can define 𝐹(𝑧) = ∫𝑧𝑓(𝑤) 𝑑𝑤. Our next goal will be to show that 𝐹is also holomorphic.

the existence of an antiderivative.

2 Cauchy's Integral Theorem
2.1
CAUCHY'S INTEGRAL FORMULA
The next question we would like to address is what happens with path integrals when a function is not holomorphic
at a point enclosed by the path. The prototype computation is
∫

which we generalize as follows.
Theorem 2.1.1 (Cauchy Integral Formula for Disks) Let 𝑓be holomorphic in a domain 𝑈, and assume that 𝐷𝑟(𝑧0) ⊂
𝑈. Then for any 𝑎∈𝐷𝑟(𝑧0),

∂𝐷𝑟(𝑧0)
𝑓(𝑧)
⁄
𝑧−𝑎𝑑𝑧
Figure 2.1
Localizing an integral
Proof.We first use a homotopy to localize the path near 𝑎, replacing the integral over ∂𝐷𝑟(𝑧0) by the integral over ∂𝐷𝜖(𝑎).
There is a slight catch because the integrand is not holomorphic at 𝑎, and we only know the path independence
for holomorphic integrands in star-shaped domains. But we can add segments back and forth between the two
boundaries of the disks to the path. This doesn't affect the integral, because the new contributions cancel. It allows
us to write the path as a sum of cycles, each lying in a star-shaped domain where the integrand is holomorphic.
Next, we rewrite the integrand so that one term becomes arbitrarily small and the other can be evaluated explicitly:
∫
∂𝐷𝑟(𝑧0)
𝑓(𝑧)

𝑓(𝑎)

For 𝜖→0, the first integrand is bounded, because we assume that 𝑓is holomorphic at 𝑎. Thus, the first integral
converges to 0 for 𝜖→0. The second integral evaluates to 2𝜋𝑖⋅𝑓(𝑎). This proves the claim.

This result is quite remarkable. It means that if we know a holomorphic function in a disk is already determined
by its values on the boundary of the disk.

Definition 2.3.1 An entire function is a function that is holomorphic in all of ℂ.

2.4
CONVERGENCE OF SEQUENCES OF HOLOMORPHIC FUNCTIONS
Definition 2.4.1
Let 𝑓𝑛be a sequence of complex-valued functions in a domain 𝑈. We say that 𝑓𝑛converges locally
uniformly to a complex valued function 𝑓in 𝑈if for every 𝑧0 ∈𝑈there is a neighborhood 𝐷⊂𝑈of 𝑧0 such that 𝑓𝑛
converges uniformly to 𝑓in 𝐷, i.e. such that for every 𝜖> 0 there is an index 𝑛0 such that for all 𝑛≥𝑛0 and all 𝑧∈𝐷we
have
| 𝑓(𝑧) −𝑓𝑛(𝑧)| < 𝜖.
In Real Analysis, we learn that if 𝑓𝑛converges to 𝑓uniformly and the functions 𝑓𝑛are continuous, then so is 𝑓.This
also holds obviously for locally uniform convergence. However, it is not generally true that a uniformly convergent
sequence of differentiable functions converges to a differentiable function. However, we will show that the locally
uniform limit of holomorphic functions is again holomorphic. To see this, we need suitable estimates for the derivatives
of a holomorphic function. These are given by the Cauchy estimates, which strengthen our previous inequalities 2.2.4:
Theorem 2.4.2 (Cauchy Estimates) Let 𝐷𝑟(𝑧0) ⊂𝐷𝑅(𝑧0) ⊂𝑈, and let 𝑓∈𝒪(𝑈). Then
∣𝑓(𝑛)(𝑧)∣≤𝐶𝑛
max
𝑤∈∂𝐷𝑅(𝑧0) | 𝑓(𝑤)|
for all 𝑧∈𝐷𝑟(𝑧0), where
𝐶𝑛=
𝑅𝑛!
⁄
(𝑅−𝑟)𝑛+1
is independent of 𝑓.
Observe that we are estimating derivatives of a function in a small disk by its values on the boundary of a larger
disk. This is a very typical situation.

Exercise 2.4.5 Prove Weierstrass Theorem 2.4.4 without using Morera's Theorem 2.3.4.
We can turn 𝒪(𝑈) into a topological vector space by introducing a family of semi-norms: For each compact disk
𝐷⊂𝑈and each index 𝑘, define
| 𝑓|𝐷,𝑘= max

∣𝑓(𝑘)(𝑧)∣.
These semi-norms generate a topology of locally uniform convergence. The corresponding uniform structure is com­
plete by Weierstrass' theorem 2.4.4.
2.5

By the inverse function theorem, we know that if 𝑧0 is a point with 𝑓′(𝑧0) ≠0, the holomorphic function 𝑓maps a
neighborhood of 𝑓bijectively and conformally onto a disk. We are now going to analyze the situation when 𝑓′(𝑧0) = 0.
Without loss, we can assume that 𝑓(𝑧0) = 0, so that we will be looking at holomorphic functions with zeroes of higher
order. Our goal is to find a normal form for them. In preparation, we make sure that we can extract square roots:

Corollary 2.5.4
Let 𝑧0 be a root of order 𝑘of the holomorphic function 𝑓. Then there is a neighborhood 𝑈of 𝑧0 that is

Proof. Write 𝑓(𝑧) = 𝑔(𝑧)𝑘where 𝑔is a function with a simple zero at 𝑧0. By the inverse function theorem applied to 𝑔,

Theorem 2.5.5 (Identity Principle) Let 𝑓be holomorphic in the connected domain 𝑈, and assume that {𝑧: 𝑓(𝑧) = 0}
has an accumulation point in 𝑈. Then 𝑓≡0 in 𝑈.
Proof. Let 𝑧0 be such an accumulation point. Then 𝑓(𝑧0) = 0. We first show that the order of 𝑓at 𝑧0 cannot be finite.
If that is the case, then there is a neighborhood of 𝑧0 where the only zero of 𝑓is at 𝑧0, contradicting that 𝑧0 is an
accumulation point of zeroes.
Now suppose that the order of the zero of 𝑓is not finite. Then all coefficients of its power series expansion are 0,
and 𝑓≡0 in a neighborhood of 𝑧0. This shows that the set of all zeroes of infinite order of 𝑓is nonempty, open, and
trivially closed. As 𝑈is connected, we are done.
Theorem 2.5.6 (Open Mapping Theorem) If 𝑓∈𝒪(𝑈) is not constant, then 𝑓(𝑈) is a domain.
Proof. Clearly, if 𝑓(𝑈) is connected, as 𝑓is continuous. To show that 𝑓(𝑈) is open, let 𝑤0 = 𝑓(𝑧0) ∈𝑓(𝑈). Then
𝑓(𝑧) −𝑤0 has a zero of finite order at 𝑧0. Thus there is 𝜖> 0 such that a neighborhood 𝑉⊂𝑈of 𝑧0 is mapped onto
𝐷𝜖(0) by 𝑓(𝑧) −𝑤0. Consequently, 𝑓maps 𝑉onto 𝐷𝜖(𝑤0), so that 𝐷𝜖(𝑤0) ⊂𝑓(𝑈).
Theorem 2.5.7 (Maximum Principle) If 𝑓∈𝒪(𝑈) is not constant, then | 𝑓(𝑧)| has no local maximum in 𝑈.
Proof. Suppose that | 𝑓(𝑧)| ≤| 𝑓(𝑧0)| for all 𝑧∈𝐷𝜖(𝑧0) ⊂𝑈. But this contradicts the Open Mapping Theorem 2.5.6,
because 𝑓(𝐷𝜖(𝑧0)) must also contain points with norm > | 𝑓(𝑧0)|.

2.6
ISOLATED SINGULARITIES
Theorem 2.6.1 (Riemann Singularity Theorem) Let 𝑓∈𝒪(𝑈−{𝑧0}), and assume that 𝑓is bounded near 𝑧0. Then
there is a function 𝑓∈𝒪(𝑈) that extends 𝑓.
Proof. Assume that 𝑧0 = 0.If 𝑓has a continuous extension to 0, then we claim we are done by Morera's theorem 2.3.4.
To see this, consider a triangle Δ ⊂𝑈. If 𝑧0 lies outside of Δ, we are done. If 𝑧0 lies on the boundary of Δ, we are
also done by continuity. If 𝑧0 lies inside Δ, cut Δ in two smaller triangles using a segment through a vertex and 𝑧0,
and reduce the problem to the previous case.
Now consider the general case and let 𝑔(𝑧) = 𝑧⋅𝑓(𝑧). This function has a continuous extension to 0. By the first
part of the proof, 𝑔extends holomorphically to 0 with 𝑔(0) = 0. Near 0, write 𝑔(𝑧) = ∑∞
𝑛=1 𝑎𝑛𝑧𝑛. Then ∑∞
𝑛=1 𝑎𝑛𝑧𝑛−1
is the desired holomorphic extension of 𝑓.
Here is a slightly more elegant alternative proof, avoiding Morera: Let ℎ(𝑧) = 𝑧2 𝑓(𝑧). Then ℎis even holomorphic
at 0, and must have a zero of order at least 2 at 0.
Definition 2.6.2 Let 𝑓∈𝒪(𝑈). An isolated point in ℂ−𝑈is called an isolated singularity of 𝑓.
We classify isolated singularities into three cases:
1. Removable singularity: 𝑓can be extended holomorphically.
2. Pole of order 𝑛: (𝑧−𝑧0)𝑛⋅𝑓(𝑧) has a removable singularity.
3. Essential singularity: otherwise.
Examples are given by 𝑓(𝑧) = 𝑧, 𝑓(𝑧) = 1/𝑧, and 𝑓(𝑧) = 𝑒1/𝑧in 𝔻−0, respectively.
Definition 2.6.3 A function 𝑓∈𝒪(𝑈) is called meromorphic if all of its isolated singularities are poles.
Recall that Cauchy's formula implies that for a holomorphic function

Exercise 3.3.1
Let 𝐴⊂ℂbe an infinite set without an accumulation point. Show that there is a sequence 𝑎𝑛∈ℂof
pairwise distinct points with 𝑎𝑛→∞such that 𝐴= {𝑎𝑛}.
Theorem 3.3.2 (Mittag-Leffler) Let 𝑎𝑛∈ℂbe pairwise distinct points without an accumulation point. Let 𝑃𝑛(𝑧) be a
polynomial of positive degree without a constant term. Then there is a meromorphic function 𝑓∈ℳ(ℂ) with poles just at
the points 𝑎𝑛, and principal parts
ℎ𝑛(𝑧) = 𝑃𝑛(

|ℎ𝑛(𝑧) −𝑄𝑛(𝑧)| < 1⁄

it has a power series expansion in that disk, which converges uniformly in any smaller disk.
Next we will show that ∑∞

Corollary 3.3.4 The field of meromorphic functions ℳ(ℂ) is the quotient field of the ring of entire functions 𝒪(ℂ).
Proof. Clearly, the quotient 𝑓/𝑔of two entire functions with 𝑔≠0 is a meromorphic function. On the other hand, given
a meromorphic function ℎ∈ℳ(ℂ), form a sequence of points 𝑎𝑛→∞enumerating the poles of 𝑚𝑛(with repetition
to obtain the correct order if needed). By the product theorem, there is a holomorphic function 𝑔∈𝒪(ℂ) with
zeroes precisely at the 𝑎𝑘. This shows that 𝑔⋅ℎ∈𝒪(ℂ).
We improve on the statement of the product theorem by providing a better estimate for the magnitude of the 𝑚𝑛to
obtain convergence. While not needed for the statement, we will make use of it when we prove the Hadamard Product
Theorem 3.6.1. The key point is to provide a better estimate for the exponential factors, which we first formulate as

3.4
THE POISSON-JENSEN FORMULA
If we are given an entire function and would like to write it as a product, the Weierstrass Product Theorem 3.3.3 does
not provide any hint on how to choose the convergence generating factors. In the second next section, we will prove
Hadamard's Product Theorem, which is a considerable improvement. In this section, we introduce entire functions of
finite growth for which this theorem is valid. A key ingredient is the following identity, which allows us to control
how quickly the zeroes of an entire function diverge to infinity.

Theorem 3.7.7 Let 𝑔(𝑧) be holomorphic in a domain 𝑈containing the vertical strip 1 ≤Re 𝑧≤2. Suppose that
1. 𝑔(𝑧+ 1) = 𝑧𝑔(𝑧) whenever defined;
2. 𝑔(𝑧) is bounded in 1 ≤Re 𝑧≤2;
3. 𝑔(1) = 1.
Then 𝑔(𝑧) = Γ(𝑧).
Proof. Denote by 𝑈𝑛the domain 𝑈translated by 𝑛∈ℤ. These domains overlap and cover all of ℂ. Let 𝑛∈ℤ≥0. For
𝑧∈𝑈𝑛, define
𝑔(𝑧) = (𝑧−1) ⋅… ⋅(𝑧−𝑛) ⋅𝑔(𝑧−𝑛) ,
and for 𝑧∈𝑈−𝑛, define
𝑔(𝑧) =
𝑔(𝑧+ 𝑛)
⁄
𝑧(𝑧+ 1) ⋅… ⋅(𝑧+ 𝑛−1) .
By property (1), this extension is well defined and holomorphic in ℂ−ℤ≤0.
In ℤ≤0, 𝑔has simple poles with the same residues as Γ, as the proof of the formula for the residues only used the
functional equation and Γ(1) = 1. Thus 𝑓(𝑧) = Γ(𝑧) −𝑔(𝑧) is an entire function with 𝑓(1) = 0.
Consider ℎ(𝑧) = 𝑓(𝑧) 𝑓(1 −𝑧). This function has period 2, because
ℎ(𝑧+ 1) = 𝑓(𝑧+ 1) 𝑓(−𝑧)
= 𝑧𝑓(𝑧) ⋅𝑓(1 −𝑧)
⁄
−𝑧
= −ℎ(𝑧) .
By assumption, | 𝑓| is bounded by a constant 𝐶in 1 ≤Re 𝑧≤2. This implies that 𝑓is bounded in 0 ≤Re 𝑧≤
1, | Im 𝑧| ≥1 as well, as
| 𝑓(𝑧)| = ∣1⁄
𝑧𝑓(𝑧+ 1)∣≤𝐶.
As 𝑓is holomorphic, it is also bounded in the compact set 0 ≤Re 𝑧≤1,| Im 𝑧| ≤1. Thus 𝑓is bounded in 0 ≤Re 𝑧≤2.
This implies that ℎis bounded in 0 ≤Re 𝑧≤2 as well, and by periodicity, in all of ℂ. By Liouville's Theorem 2.3.2, ℎ
is constant. As 𝑓(1) = 0, ℎ(𝑧) = 𝑓(𝑧) 𝑓(1 −𝑧) is identically 0. If 𝑓were not identically zero, it wouldn't vanish in
some open set. In this set, 𝑓(1 −𝑧) must vanish. Hence 𝑓(1 −𝑧) vanishes identical in all of ℂ, and so does 𝑓. This
proves 𝑔= Γ.

4 Global Complex Analysis
4.1
CAUCHY'S THEOREM
In this section, we address the following question: Let 𝑈be a domain. For which closed paths 𝛾in 𝑈do we have
∫
𝛾
𝑓(𝑧) 𝑑𝑧= 0
for all holomorphic functions 𝑓∈𝒪(𝑈)?
So far, we know that if 𝑈is star-shaped, this holds for all closed paths. But we have also seen that for 𝑈a domain
containing the unit circle but not 0,
∫
∂𝔻
1⁄
𝑧𝑑𝑧= 2𝜋
In other words, integrals over 1/𝑧detect potentials for Cauchy's integral theorem failing. We turn this into a definition.
Definition 4.1.1 Let 𝛾be a closed path, and 𝑎∉𝛾. Then
𝑛(𝛾, 𝑎) = 1
⁄
2𝜋𝑖∫
𝛾
𝑑𝑤

As a geometric motivation of the name, assume that 𝑎= 0 without loss. For two rays footed at 0 ending at 𝑧1 and 𝑧2
that are not opposites there is a unique angle 𝜙∈(−𝜋, 𝜋) such that

|𝑧2| .
We denote this angle by ∠(𝑧1, 𝑧2). Given a closed curve 𝛾: [𝑎, 𝑏] →ℂwith 0 ∉𝛾, choose a partition
𝑎= 𝑡0 < ⋯< 𝑡𝑛= 𝑏

Definition 4.1.5 A chain in a domain 𝑈is a formal finite linear combination 𝜎= ∑𝑘𝑛𝑘𝛾𝑘with 𝑛𝑘∈ℤand 𝛾𝑘a curve

Two chains 𝜎1 and 𝜎2 are called homologous if their difference 𝜎1 −𝜎2 is a null homologous cycle.

number with respect to points outside of 𝑈must be 0.
Now pick any vertex in the lattice, and consider a lattice path from that vertex to that square, around it, and back
the same way. By Cauchy's theorem 2.1.1 again, this loop has ∫𝑓(𝑧) 𝑑𝑧= 0, and its winding number with respect
to 𝑎𝑘is 1. By adding suitable multiples of such loops to 𝛾, we can eliminate all squares with non-zero winding
number without affecting ∫𝛾𝑓(𝑧) 𝑑𝑧.
Finally, any closed 𝜖-path that does not enclose any square uses all of its edges in both directions equally often. To
see this, consider a segment that is used 𝑎times in one direction, and 𝑏time in the other direction. Modify the path
along this segment so that it uses the two squares 𝐴and 𝐵adjacent to the right of the segment, respectively. Denote
the centers of these squares by 𝑣𝐴and 𝑣𝑏. Then
𝑎= 𝑛(𝛾, 𝑣𝐴) = 𝑛(𝛾, 𝑣𝐵) = 𝑏,
because there is no edge separating 𝑣𝐴and 𝑣𝐵. This proves the second claim.

4.3
COUNTING ZEROS
The moral of the Residue Theorem 4.1.9 is that everything about a holomorphic function 𝑓that can be computed as
a residue can also be computed as an integral. The question arises: which features of 𝑓can be expressed as residues?
As a first example, we use residues to count the number of zeroes.

This means that we can compute the number of zeroes of a meromorphic function 𝑓inside (say) a circle by determining

That we can express the number of zeroes of a function (which is an integer) as an integral (which depends notoriously
continuously on parameters) suggests that we look at how small distortions of holomorphic functions affect their
zeroes. This is the content of the following theorem.
Theorem 4.3.2 (Rouché) Let 𝐷⊂𝑈be a disk. Let 𝑓and 𝑔be holomorphic in 𝑈such that
|𝑔(𝑧)| < | 𝑓(𝑧)|
for all 𝑧∈∂𝐷. Then 𝑓and 𝑓+ 𝑔have the same number of zeroes in 𝐷.
Proof. For 0 ≤𝑡≤1, consider
𝑓𝑡(𝑧) = 𝑓(𝑧) + 𝑡𝑔(𝑧) .
For 𝑧∈∂𝐷we have
| 𝑓𝑡(𝑧)| = | 𝑓(𝑧) + 𝑡𝑔(𝑧)|
≥| 𝑓(𝑧)| −𝑡|𝑔(𝑧)|
≥| 𝑓(𝑧)| −|𝑔(𝑧)|
> 0 .
Thus, the number of zeroes of 𝑓+ 𝑡𝑔in 𝐷is given by the integral
1
⁄
2𝜋𝑖∫
∂𝐷
𝑓′ + 𝑡𝑔′
⁄
𝑓+ 𝑡𝑔𝑑𝑧
which is continuous in 𝑡and an integer, thus constant.
The following theorem shows that not having zeroes survives limits, unless catastrophe strikes.
Theorem 4.3.3
(Hurwitz) Let 𝑓𝑛be holomorphic and assume that 𝑓𝑛→𝑓locally uniformly in 𝑈. Assume also that
𝑓𝑛(𝑧) ≠0 in 𝑈. Then either 𝑓≡0, or 𝑓(𝑧) ≠0 for all 𝑧∈𝑈as well.
Proof. Let's assume by contradiction that 𝑓≢0 in 𝑈and that 𝑓(𝑝) = 0 for some 𝑝∈𝑈. As 𝑓≢0, we can find a disk
𝐷⊂𝑈containing 𝑝such that 𝑓(𝑧) ≠0 for all 𝑧∈∂𝐷. Then | 𝑓(𝑧)| ≥𝜖for some 𝜖> 0 and all 𝑧∈∂𝐷. As 𝑓𝑛→𝑓
locally uniformly, there is an index 𝑛such that
| 𝑓𝑛(𝑧) −𝑓(𝑧)| < 𝜖< | 𝑓(𝑧)|
for all 𝑧∈∂𝐷. By Rouché's Theorem 4.3.2, 𝑓and 𝑓𝑛have the same number of zeroes in 𝐷. But this means that 𝑓𝑛
also has a zero in 𝐷, contradicting the assumption.
Corollary 4.3.4 Let 𝑓𝑛be holomorphic and assume that 𝑓𝑛→𝑓locally uniformly in 𝑈. Assume also that 𝑓𝑛(𝑧) is injective
in 𝑈. Then either 𝑓≡0, or 𝑓(𝑧) is injective as well.
Proof. Assume 𝑓(𝑧1) = 𝑤= 𝑓(𝑧2), and let 𝐷𝜖(𝑧1) and 𝐷𝜖(𝑧2) be disjoint disks in which 𝑓assumes the value 𝑤only
once each at 𝑧1 and 𝑧2, respsectively. Then

Corollary 4.3.5 Let 𝑓𝑛be holomorphic and assume that 𝑓𝑛→𝑓locally uniformly in 𝑈. Assume also that 𝑓𝑛(𝑧) assumes

so that by Rouché's Theorem 4.3.2, 𝑓𝑛and 𝑓have the same number of zeroes in ⋃𝐷𝑘.
This also reproves the injectivity statement above.
4.4
RUNGE THEORY
The goal of this section is to approximate holomorphic functions on compact sets by rational functions and polynomials.
The proof of Runge's first theorem has two key ingredients: We represent the holomorphic function on 𝐾by an integral
over a path enclosing the compact set, and we approximate this integral by Riemann sums.

PDF PAGE 74
5 Conformal Mappings
In this chapter, we study Geometric Function Theory, which is concerned with the behavior of holomorphic functions as
maps between domains in the plane. We know already that such maps are conformal and map open sets to open sets.
5.1
MONTEL'S THEOREM
Definition 5.1.1 A sequence 𝑓𝑛∈𝒪(𝑈) is locally uniformly bounded if for every point 𝑝∈𝑈there is a disk 𝐷𝑟(𝑝)
and a constant 𝐶such that
| 𝑓𝑛(𝑧)| ≤𝐶
for all 𝑧∈𝐷𝑟(𝑝).
Note that this definition allows that each individual 𝑓𝑛is unbounded in 𝑈.
Definition 5.1.2 A sequence 𝑓𝑛∈𝒪(𝑈) is locally equicontinuous if for every point 𝑝∈𝑈there is a disk 𝐷𝑟(𝑝) such
that the sequence 𝑓𝑛is equicontinuous in 𝐷𝑟(𝑝), i.e. such that for every 𝜖> 0 there is a 𝛿> 0 such that for all 𝑛and all
𝑧, 𝑧′ ∈𝐷𝑟(𝑝) with |𝑧−𝑧′| < 𝛿we have | 𝑓𝑛(𝑧) −𝑓𝑛(𝑧′)| < 𝜖.
Lemma 5.1.3 Let 𝑄⊂𝑈be a countable and dense subset, and let 𝑓𝑛be a pointwise bounded sequence of functions in 𝑈.
Then 𝑓𝑛has a subsequence that converges on 𝑄.
Proof.
Let {𝑞𝑛} be an enumeration of 𝑄. Choose subsequence 𝑓1
𝑛of 𝑓the converges at 𝑞1. Continue inductively to
choose subsequences 𝑓𝑘+1
𝑛
of 𝑓𝑘
𝑛that converge at 𝑞1, … , 𝑞𝑘+1. Then the diagonal sequence 𝑓𝑛
𝑛converges on 𝑄.
Theorem 5.1.4
(Arzela-Ascoli) Let 𝑓𝑛be pointwise bounded and locally equicontinuous in 𝑈. Then 𝑓𝑛has a locally
uniform convergent subsequence.
Proof. By the lemma, we can assume that 𝑓𝑛converges on 𝑄= ℚ2 ∩𝑈. Let 𝑝∈𝑈, and 𝐷= 𝐷𝑟(𝑝) ⊂𝑈be a disk on

Corollary 5.1.6 (Montel) A locally uniformly bounded sequence of holomorphic functions has a locally uniformly con­
vergent subsequence.
Under the mild additional assumption that the sequence of functions converges on a set with an accumulation point,
we can avoid taking subsequences:
Corollary 5.1.7
(Vitali) Let 𝑓𝑛be a locally uniformly bounded sequence of holomorphic functions in a domain 𝑈. Let
𝑧𝑘∈𝑈converge to 𝑧0 ∈𝑈, where 𝑧0 ≠𝑧𝑘. Assume that for all 𝑘the sequence 𝑓𝑛(𝑧𝑘) converges. Then 𝑓𝑛converges locally
uniformly in 𝑈.
Proof. By Montel's theorem, there is a subsequence of the 𝑓𝑛that converges uniformly. Thus, it suffices to show that
any two convergent subsequences have the same limit. But these two limits agree at the points 𝑧𝑘. By the Identity
Principle 2.5.5, the limits agree in all of 𝑈.
As an application, we show a general result about integrals of holomorphic functions that depend continuously on
a parameter.

5.2
THE RIEMANN MAPPING THEOREM
In this section, we will prove the Riemann mapping theorem that says that all domains “without holes” are either
the complex plane ℂor biholomorphic to the unit disk. The intuitive notion “without holes” is made precise by the
concept of a simply connected domain. In topology, one defines:
Definition 5.2.1 A connected domain 𝑈is simply connected if every closed curve in 𝑈is null homologous.
Here are two equivalent notions. We claim
Theorem 5.2.2 For a connected domain 𝑈⊂ℂthe following are equivalent:
1. Every closed curve in 𝑈is null homotopic;
2. 𝑈is simply connected;
3.
̂ℂ−𝑈is connected.
Proof. We first prove 1 ⇒2. If 𝑈is simply connected, then trivially every closed curve in 𝑈has winding number zero
with respect to any point outside 𝑈, as the winding number does not change under homotopies that don't cross the
reference point. This shows 1 ⇒2.

PDF PAGE 77
The Riemann Mapping Theorem
Conformal Mappings
76
Figure 5.1
Constructing a curve with positive winding number
For 2 ⇒3, let's assume that
̂ℂ−𝑈is not connected. Let 𝐵be a component of
̂ℂ−𝑈that does not contain ∞.
Then 𝐵is bounded and closed, and hence compact. It therefore has a positive distance 3𝜖to its complement in
̂ℂ−𝑈. Overlay ℂwith an 𝜖-grid of horizontal and vertical lines, and let 𝑆𝑖be the (finitely many) grid 𝜖× 𝜖squares
that cover 𝐵. The boundary 𝛾of their union lies in 𝑈. Pick a point 𝑏∈𝐵that is on no grid line (which can be
achieved by shifting the grid if necessary). Then 𝑏∈𝑆𝑖for precisely one index 𝑖, so that the winding number of the
cycle 𝑐= ∑𝑖∂𝑆𝑖with respect to 𝑏is one. That cycle 𝑐, however, has the same winding number with respect to 𝑏as 𝛾.
Hence 𝛾is not null homologous in 𝑈.
For 3 ⇒2, we can assume
̂ℂ−𝑈contains not just ∞. Then note that 𝑛(𝛾, 𝑝) = 0 for any cycle 𝛾in 𝑈and 𝑝∈ℂ−𝑈
sufficiently large. As the winding number is continuous in 𝑝as long as 𝑝stays away from 𝛾, 𝑛(𝛾, 𝑝) is constant in
̂ℂ−𝑈, and hence identically 0, so that 𝛾is null homologous.
To prove 2 ⇒1, we will use the Riemann mapping theorem. The claim follows, because both ℂand 𝔻are simply
connected.

5.3
EXTENSION TO THE BOUNDARY
The Riemann mapping function provides us with a biholomorphic map between the interiors of two domains, but
nothing is said about a possible extension to the boundaries. That this can be problematic can be seen in simple

PDF PAGE 80
Extension to the Boundary
Conformal Mappings
79
domains with slits.
There is an extensive theory about the boundary behavior of holomorphic maps. For bounded domains, the best
result is due to CarathÃ©odory.
Definition 5.3.1 A Jordan curve is the continuous and injective image of a circle. A Jordan domain is a connected domain
bounded by a Jordan curve.
By the Jordan Curve Theorem, any Jordan domain is simply connected.
Theorem 5.3.2 (CarathÃ©odory) Let 𝑈and 𝑉be Jordan domains, and 𝑓: 𝑈→𝑉be a biholomorphic map. Then 𝑓has
a unique extension to a homeomorphism
̅𝑓: 𝑈→𝑉.
The proof requires specialized tools from planar topology and is technical. We will prove the following special case,
which is good enough for all applications we have in mind, and shows the basic idea.
Theorem 5.3.3 Let 𝑈and 𝑉be domains with piecewise smooth boundary, and 𝑓: 𝑈→𝑉a biholomorphic map. Then 𝑓
has a unique continuous extension to the boundary.
Proof. We will show that 𝑓is uniformly continuous in 𝑈. Then the existence of the extension follows from point-set
topology: a uniformly continuous map from a subset 𝑈of a metric space into a complete metric space has a unique
continuous extension to the closure of 𝑈.
Assume by contradiction that 𝑓is not uniformly continuous in 𝑈. Then there exists 𝜖> 0 such that for all 𝑛> 0
there are 𝑧𝑛, 𝑧′𝑛∈𝑈with |𝑧𝑛−𝑧′𝑛| < 1⁄
𝑛and | 𝑓(𝑧𝑛) −𝑓(𝑧′𝑛)| > 3𝜖. By compactness of 𝑈, we can assume that 𝑧𝑛and
𝑧′𝑛converge to a common limit 𝑧0 ∈∂𝑈.
By compactness of 𝑉we can also assume that the sequences 𝑤𝑛= 𝑓(𝑧𝑛) and 𝑤′𝑛= 𝑓(𝑧′𝑛) converge to different points
𝑤0, 𝑤′0 with |𝑤0 −𝑤′0| ≥3𝜖. As 𝑓: 𝑈→𝑉is in particular a homeomorphism, we necessarily have that 𝑤0, 𝑤′0 ∈∂𝑉.
Denote by 𝐷= 𝐷𝜖(𝑤0) and 𝐷′ = 𝐷𝜖(𝑤′0) two disks centered at 𝑤0 and 𝑤′0 distance 𝜖apart.
Because the boundary of 𝑉is piecewise smooth, we can find continuous curves 𝜂, 𝜂′ : [0, 1) →𝑉and a monotone
sequence 𝑡𝑛∈[0, 1) with 𝑡𝑛→1 such that 𝑤𝑛= 𝜂(𝑡𝑛) and 𝑤′𝑛= 𝜂′(𝑡𝑛). Let 𝛾= 𝑓−1 ∘𝜂and 𝛾′ = 𝑓−1 ∘𝜂′ the
preimages in 𝑈. By construction, 𝛾(𝑡𝑛) = 𝑧𝑛and 𝛾′(𝑡𝑛) = 𝑧′𝑛. In particular, both 𝛾and 𝛾′ have points that are
arbitrarily close to 𝑧0. Fix 𝑅> 0 so that 𝛾(0) and 𝛾′(0) lie outside 𝐷𝑅= 𝐷𝑅(𝑧0) ∩𝑈. For 0 < 𝑟< 𝑅, denote by 𝑧𝑟
(resp. 𝑧′𝑟) an intersection of 𝛾(resp. 𝛾′) with ∂𝐷𝑟(𝑧0).

5.4
THE SCHWARZ-CHRISTOFFEL FORMULA
The proof of the Riemann Mapping Theorem 5.2.6 does not indicate how to construct the mapping function explicitly.
There is an important special case where this is possible, namely when the domain is a Euclidean polygon.
We begin with a preparation.

Theorem 5.4.1 (Schwarz Reflection Principle) Let 𝑈⊂ℍbe a domain and (𝑎, 𝑏) ⊂ℝ∩∂𝑈a piece of the boundary
of 𝑈that lies on the real axis. Assume that 𝑓is holomorphic in 𝑈, continuous in 𝑈∪(𝑎, 𝑏), and real valued on (𝑎, 𝑏). Then
𝑔(𝑧) =
{
𝑓(𝑧)
if 𝑧∈𝑈∪(𝑎, 𝑏)
𝑓( ̅𝑧)
if 𝑧∈𝑈
extends 𝑓to the domain 𝑉= 𝑈∪(𝑎, 𝑏) ∪𝑈.
Proof. We only have to show that 𝑓is holomorphic at points in (𝑎, 𝑏). For 𝑧0 ∈(𝑎, 𝑏), let 𝐷𝜖(𝑧0) ⊂𝑉be a disk contained
in 𝑉. We show that 𝑔is holomorphic in 𝐷𝜖(𝑧0). Let Δ ⊂𝐷𝜖(𝑧0) be a triangle. To see that ∫∂Δ𝑔(𝑧) 𝑑𝑧= 0, note that at
worst (𝑎, 𝑏) cuts Δ into two polygons Δ1 and Δ2. The function 𝑔is holomorphic in the interior of Δ𝑗and continuous
in Δ𝑗, so that ∫∂Δ𝑗𝑔(𝑧) 𝑑𝑧= 0. Thus 𝑔is holomorphic in 𝐷𝜖(𝑧0) by Morera's Theorem 2.3.4.
Definition 5.4.2 A Euclidean polygon is a bounded simply connected domain in the plane such that all boundary com­
ponents consist of straight segments, meeting in a finite set of vertices.
Theorem 5.4.3 (Schwarz-Christoffel Formula) Let 𝑃be a Euclidean polygon with vertices at points 𝑣𝑘, 𝑘= 1, … , 𝑛,

Proof.

6 Elliptic Functions
6.1
ELLIPTIC FUNCTIONS
Definition 6.1.1 A lattice Λ is given by
Λ = {𝑛1𝜔1 + 𝑛2𝜔2 : 𝑛1, 𝑛2 ∈ℤ}
with generators 𝜔1,𝜔2 ∈ℂlinearly independent over ℝ. The two generators form a basis of the lattice. The parallelogram Π
spanned by 𝜔1 and 𝜔2 is called a fundamental parallelogram of Λ.
Note that neither the basis nor the fundamental parallelogram is uniquely determined by a lattice.
Example 6.1.1 The square lattice can be generated by 1 and 𝑖. The hexagonal lattice can be generated by 1 and 1⁄
2 (1 + √
⁡
3𝑖).
Definition 6.1.2 A function 𝑓is called elliptic with respect to the lattice Λ if it is meromorphic in ℂand satisfies 𝑓(𝑧+𝜔) =
𝑓(𝑧) for all 𝜔∈Λ. We denote the field of elliptic functions with respect to Λ by ℰ(Λ).
Note that derivatives of elliptic functions are again elliptic. An elliptic function has only finitely many zeroes and
poles within a fundamental parallelogram.
Definition 6.1.3 Let 𝑓∈ℰ(Λ) be elliptic, and Π a translated fundamental parallelogram so that no poles of 𝑓lie on ∂Π.
Then the order of 𝑓is the number of its poles in Π, counted with multiplicity.
Lemma 6.1.4 Elliptic functions of order 0 are constant.
Proof. An elliptic function of order 0 is holomorphic, hence entire. As Π is compact, it is bounded. Liouville's Theo­
rem 2.3.2 shows it is constant.
Theorem 6.1.5 Let 𝑓∈ℰ(Λ) with poles 𝑤1, … , 𝑤𝑛∈Π. Then
𝑛
∑
𝑘=1
res𝑤𝑘𝑓= 0 .
Proof. By the residue theorem,
𝑛
∑

res𝑤𝑘𝑓= 1
⁄
2𝜋𝑖∫
∂Π
𝑓(𝑧) 𝑑𝑧
= 0
by periodicity, as integrals over opposite edges cancel.
Corollary 6.1.6 There are no elliptic functions of order 1.

Proof. Such a function would have a single pole of order 1. Such a pole has a non-zero residue.

Proof. Translate Π so that 𝑓does not assume 𝑤on ∂Π. Then

Definition 7.1.1 A Hermitian metric on a domain 𝑈is a 𝐶2 function 𝜌: 𝑈→ℝ>0 which vanishes only at isolated points.
We denote a domain with a Hermitian metric by (𝑈, 𝜌).

An important method to construct new Hermitian metrics is by pull-back:

Theorem 7.3.3 An entire bounded function is constant.
Proof. Suppose 𝑓: ℂ→𝔻is an entire, non-constant function. Then 𝜌= 𝑓∗𝜌𝔻is a Hermitian metric with 𝐾𝜌= −1,

Note that this proof makes no use of the Cauchy integral theorem. In fact, we only need the Cauchy-Riemann

In the next section, we will need a version of Ahlfors' Lemma 7.3.1 where the Hermitian metric is only continuous.
The condition of curvature is implemented through a supporting metric:
Definition 7.3.4 An upper semicontinuous Hermitian metric 𝜌: 𝑈→ℝ≥0 on a domain 𝑈is called ultrahyperbolic if
for each 𝑝∈𝑈with 𝜌(𝑝) > 0 there is a Hermitian metric 𝜌𝑝: 𝑈𝑝→ℝ>0 of class 𝐶2 defined in a neighborhood 𝑈𝑝of 𝑝such
that for 𝑧∈𝑈𝑝
1. 𝐾𝜌𝑝(𝑧) ≤−1;
2. 𝜌𝑝(𝑧) ≤𝜌(𝑧);
3. 𝜌𝑝(𝑝) = 𝜌(𝑝).
The metric 𝜌𝑝is called a supporting metric of 𝜌at 𝑝.
We can now formulate a refined version of Ahlfors' Lemma 7.3.1:
Theorem 7.3.5 (Ahlfors' Lemma) Let 𝜌be an ultrahyperbolic metric in 𝔻. Then
𝜌≤𝜆𝔻.

We have to show that 𝑀≤1. In case that 𝜌(𝑝) = 0, we have 𝑀= 0 and are done.
Otherwise, let 𝜌𝑝be a supporting metric for 𝜌at 𝑝, defined in a neighborhood 𝑈𝑝of 𝑝. Let

7.4
LANDAU-SCHOTTKY THEORY
The goal of this section is to prove the following two theorems:

Theorem 7.4.1
(Landau) There is a universal constant 𝐿such that that for 𝑓∈𝒪(𝔻) which is normalized so that
𝑓(0) = 0 and 𝑓′(0) = 1, 𝑓(𝔻) contains a disk of radius at least 𝐿. The smallest such 𝐿is called Landau's constant.
Theorem 7.4.2 (Bloch) There is a universal constant 𝐵such that for 𝑓∈𝒪(𝔻) which is normalized so that 𝑓(0) = 0
and 𝑓′(0) = 1, 𝑓(𝔻) contains a disk of radius at least 𝐿that is the injective image of a subset of 𝔻.
Clearly, we have 0 ≤𝐵≤𝐿≤1. The optimal values for 𝐿and 𝐵are not known. The Ahlfors-Grunsky conjecture

Let 𝑓: 𝔻→𝑈be a surjective holomorphic map. For 𝑝∈𝔻, let 𝑞= 𝑓(𝑝), and define 𝜎(𝑝) to be the largest
number such that 𝐷𝜎(𝑝)(𝑞) is biholomorphic image of an open neighborhood 𝑈𝑝of 𝑝in 𝔻.
In case that 𝑓′(𝑝) = 0, we set 𝜎(𝑝) = 0.
We clearly have 𝜎(𝑝) ≤𝐵𝑓. Let 𝑔: 𝐷𝜎(𝑝)(𝑞) →𝑈𝑝be the inverse of 𝑓.
2. The boundary of 𝑈𝑝
We claim that the boundary of 𝑈𝑝contains points of ∂𝔻or points with 𝑓′(𝑧) =
0. Assume the contrary, and let 𝑤∈∂𝐷𝜎(𝑝)(𝑞). Choose a sequence 𝑤𝑛∈𝐷𝜎(𝑝)(𝑞) with 𝑤𝑛→𝑤0, and let
𝑧𝑛= 𝑔(𝑤𝑛). After choosing a subsequence, we can assume that 𝑧𝑛→𝑧∈∂𝑈𝑝⊂𝔻. We then have 𝑓(𝑧) = 𝑤
and 𝑓′(𝑧) ≠0. Choose a small disk 𝐷𝜖(𝑤) on which 𝑓−1 exists with 𝑓−1(𝑤) = 𝑧. On 𝐷𝜎(𝑝)(𝑞) ∩𝐷𝜖(𝑤) we
then have 𝑓−1 = 𝑔, so we have a holomorphic extension of 𝑔to 𝐷𝜎(𝑝)(𝑞) ∪𝐷𝜖(𝑤). Now cover ∂𝐷𝜎(𝑝)(𝑞) with
finitely many such disks, and extend 𝑔holomorphically to all of them. This way we find a disk 𝐷𝑠(𝑞) with
𝑠> 𝜎(𝑝) on which 𝑓−1 is defined and extends 𝑔, a contradiction to the definition of 𝜎.
3. Continuity of 𝜎
We claim that 𝜎: 𝔻→[0, 𝐵𝑓] is a continuous function. Moreover, near points 𝑝with
𝑓′(𝑝) = 0, we have explicitly that
𝜎(𝑧) = | 𝑓(𝑧) −𝑓(𝑝)| .
Suppose first that 𝜎(𝑝) ≠0, and let 𝑞= 𝑓(𝑝). For 𝑤∈𝐷𝜎(𝑝)(𝑞) and 𝑧= 𝑔(𝑤) ∈𝑈𝑝we have
𝜎(𝑝) −|𝑤−𝑞| ≤𝜎(𝑧) ≤𝜎(𝑝) + |𝑤−𝑞|
by triangle inequality and the definition of 𝜎. This shows
lim

7.5
UNIVALENT FUNCTION
Definition 7.5.1 A function 𝑓∈𝒪(𝑈) is called univalent or schlicht if it is injective.
By the Open Mapping Theorem 2.5.6, a univalent function is a biholomorphic map 𝑓: 𝑈→𝑓(𝑈). We also note
that necessarily 𝑓′(𝑧) ≠0 in 𝑈.
In this section, we will discuss univalent functions 𝑓∈𝒪(𝔻). Denote by
𝒮= { 𝑓∈𝒪(𝔻) : 𝑓(0) = 0, 𝑓′(0) = 1, and 𝑓is univalent}

In 1916, Bieberbach went on to conjecture that |𝑎𝑛| ≤𝑛for all 𝑛. This was the (infamous) Bieberbach conjecture.
–
1916: |𝑎2| ≤2 (Bieberbach)
–
1923: |𝑎3| ≤3 (Löwner)
–
1955: |𝑎4| ≤4 (Garabedian and Schiffer)
–
1969: |𝑎6| ≤6 (Ozawa)
–
1972: |𝑎5| ≤5 (Pederson)
Then, in 1985, De Branges proved the general case. This was politically charged: De Branges had earlier attempted
to prove the Bieberbach conjecture, but his proof was found to be incorrect. Nobody in the West was willing to read his
new complicated manuscript. So at the height of the Cold War, he went to (then) Leningrad and discussed the proof
in detail with Russian experts. They believed him. Only then were experts in the West willing to follow his arguments.

An important consequence of the distortion theorem is that univalent functions in the unit disk are locally uniformly
bounded. We will make use of this in the next section.
7.6
CARATHÉODORY KERNEL CONVERGENCE
Definition 7.6.1 Let 𝐹𝑛be a sequence of domains in ℂwith 0 ∈𝐹𝑛. The kernel of the sequence 𝐹𝑛is the set 𝐹consisting
of the point 0 and all points 𝑧with the property such that there is a domain 𝑈containing 0 and 𝑧that is contained in all 𝐹𝑛
for sufficiently large 𝑛:
{0, 𝑧} ⊂𝑈⊂𝐹𝑛
for 𝑛large enough.
If there are no such points, the kernel 𝐹is just the set {0}. Otherwise, each set 𝑈is contained in 𝐹, which is therefore a domain
containing 0. We say that 𝐹𝑛converges to 𝐹if all subsequences of 𝐹𝑛have the same kernel.
Example 7.6.1
Let 𝐹𝑛be a decreasing sequence of domains, and let 𝐹∘be the set of interior points of the intersection
⋃𝐹𝑛. Then 𝐹𝑛converges to the component 𝐹of 𝐹∘that contains 0, and to 𝐹= {0} if 𝐹∘does not contain 0.
Proof. As taking a subsequence doesn't change 𝐹∘, it suffices to show that the kernel of 𝐹𝑛is 𝐹. If 0 ∈𝐹∘, then clearly 𝐹is
contained in the kernel. Let 𝑧be a point in the kernel. Then there is a domain 𝑈containing 0 and 𝑧that is contained
in all 𝐹𝑛by monotonicity. Therefore 𝑈⊂𝐹∘. As 𝑈contains 0 and is connected, 𝑈⊂𝐹. If 0 ∉𝐹∘, then no point 𝑧≠0
can be in the kernel, as it would then contain 0 as an interior point, and thus 0 ∈𝐹∘.
Example 7.6.2 Let
𝐹𝑛= ℂ−{1 + 𝑖𝑡: |𝑡| ≥1⁄
𝑛}
be the plane with two approaching vertical slits removed. Then 𝐹𝑛converges to Re 𝑧< 1.
The main reason to introduce kernel convergence is the following characterization of locally uniform convergence
of univalent functions:
Theorem 7.6.2
(Carathéodory) Let 𝑓𝑛∈𝒪(𝔻) be univalent and normalized so that 𝑓𝑛(0) = 0 and 𝑓′𝑛(0) > 0. Let
𝐹𝑛= 𝑓(𝔻). Then the sequence 𝑓𝑛converges locally uniformly if and only if the sequence of domains 𝐹𝑛converges to its
kernel 𝐹≠ℂ. In that case, 𝐹is the image domain of the limit function 𝑓.
Proof. First, suppose that 𝑓𝑛→𝑓locally uniformly in 𝔻, and let 𝐹be the kernel of 𝐹𝑛. We claim that 𝐹= 𝑓(𝔻). By
Hurwitz' Theorem 4.3.3, 𝑓is either univalent or constant.

PDF PAGE 111
Carathéodory Kernel Convergence
Geometric Function Theory
110
We show first that 𝑓(𝔻) ⊂𝐹. If 𝑓is identically 0, this is obvious. Hence, we can assume that 𝑓is injective. Let
𝑓(𝑧0) = 𝑤0 ∈𝑓(𝔻). If 𝑤0 = 0, then clearly also 𝑤0 ∈𝐹, so let's assume 𝑤0 ≠0. Choose 𝑟such that 0 < |𝑧0| < 𝑟< 1.
Then the domain 𝑈= 𝑓(|𝑧| < 𝑟) contains 𝑤0 and 0. We claim that 𝑈⊂𝐹𝑛for 𝑛sufficiently large. If this is false, then
there are sequences 𝑛𝑘→∞and 𝑤𝑘∈𝑈with 𝑤𝑘∉𝐹𝑛𝑘. After another subsequence, we can assume that 𝑤𝑘→𝑤
with 𝑤∈𝑈. As 𝑓𝑛𝑘(𝑧) ≠𝑤𝑘for all 𝑧∈𝔻, we also have 𝑓𝑛𝑘(𝑧)−𝑤𝑘+𝑤≠𝑤for all 𝑧∈𝔻. By Hurwitz' Theorem 4.3.3,
we have 𝑓(𝑧) ≠𝑤in 𝔻, as 𝑓is not constant. But this contradicts 𝑤∈𝑈⊂𝑓(𝔻).
Now we show that 𝐹⊂𝑓(𝔻). Let 0 ≠𝑤0 ∈𝐹. Then there is an open set 𝑈⊂𝐹𝑛for large 𝑛that contains 0 and 𝑤0.
Let 𝑔𝑛: 𝑈→𝔻the inverse of 𝑓𝑛. By Montel's Theorem 5.1.6, there is a subsequence 𝑔𝑛𝑘that converges in 𝑈locally
uniformly to a limit 𝑔. This limit satisfies 𝑔(0) = 0 and |𝑔(𝑤)| ≤1, and hence |𝑔(𝑤)| < 1 by the Open Mapping
Theorem 2.5.6. Thus 𝑓𝑛𝑘(𝑧) converges uniformly in a neighborhood of 𝑧0 = 𝑔(𝑤0). As 𝑔𝑛𝑘(𝑤0) →𝑔(𝑤0) = 𝑧0, we
obtain 𝑤0 = 𝑓𝑛𝑘(𝑔𝑛𝑘(𝑤0)) →𝑓(𝑧0) so that 𝑤0 ∈𝑓(𝔻).
This shows that 𝐹= 𝑓(𝔻). As 𝑓is injective or constant, it follows also that 𝐹≠ℂ.
Now suppose that 𝐹𝑛converges to its kernel 𝐹≠ℂ. By Koebe's One Quarter Theorem 7.5.7,
{𝑤: |𝑤| ≤1⁄
4 𝑓′𝑛(0)} ⊂𝐹𝑛.
If 𝑓′𝑛(0) is unbounded, a subsequence of 𝐹𝑛will have ℂas its kernel, contradicting 𝐹≠ℂ. Hence, we can assume
that 𝑓′𝑛(0) ≤𝐶. By the Distortion Theorem 7.5.8,
| 𝑓𝑛(𝑧)| ≤𝐶
|𝑧|
⁄
(1 −|𝑧|)2
for all 𝑧∈𝔻, so that 𝑓𝑛is locally uniformly bounded. Thus 𝑓𝑛has a locally uniform convergent subsequence.
In order to show that 𝑓𝑛itself already converges locally uniformly, it suffices to show that any two locally uniform
convergent subsequences have the same limit. Suppose that is not the case, and we have two such subsequences 𝑓𝑚𝑘
and 𝑓𝑛𝑘with different limits 𝑓and 𝑔. By the first part of the theorem (which we have already shown above), the
domains 𝐹𝑚𝑘and 𝐹𝑛𝑘converge to the images 𝑓(𝔻) and 𝑔(𝔻). By assumption, both domain sequences have the
same kernel, so that 𝑓(𝔻) = 𝑔(𝔻). By the uniqueness of the Riemann mapping function, 𝑓= 𝑔.
